<hgroup><h1>Chronic spontaneous urticaria: update on pathogenesis and therapeutic implications<sup><a href="#d36e1116" class="usa-link">؆</a></sup>
</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Criado%20PR"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Paulo Ricardo Criado</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">

<div class="author-notes p"><div class="fn" id="cor0005">
<sup>⁎</sup><p class="display-inline">Corresponding author. <span>prcriado@uol.com.br</span></p>
</div></div>

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2024 Nov 16; Accepted 2025 Jan 29; Issue date 2025 Sep-Oct.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2025 Sociedade Brasileira de Dermatologia. Published by Elsevier España, S.L.U.</div>
<p>This is an open access article under the CC BY license (http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC12539477  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40845419/" class="usa-link">40845419</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abs0005"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Background</h3>
<p>The understanding of chronic spontaneous urticaria pathogenesis has been increasing recently. The central role of mast cells is being reinforced, but multiple cells, pathways, and mediators are involved in a complex interrelationship. Modern therapies for its management reflect the need to encompass different mechanisms and promise to alter the course of urticaria and the long journey of those with refractory disease. Continuous updating of these aspects is necessary to optimize patient care.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Objectives</h3>
<p>To review concepts and advances in the pathogenesis of chronic spontaneous urticaria, in addition to contextualizing promising drug options for its management.</p></section><section id="sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Method</h3>
<p>A narrative review was conducted between 1977 and 2024, including relevant articles published in the scientific literature, indexed in the PubMed system.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h3>
<p>A total of 25,732 articles were found. Inclusion criteria were determined by the authors' decision regarding their level of importance for furthering knowledge in the areas of pathogenesis and treatment of chronic spontaneous urticaria, with preference given to meta-analyses, systematic reviews, and randomized trials. Regarding therapeutics, 138 articles from the last 15 years were prioritized, in addition to records on ClinicalTrials.gov, and the drugs could be in the clinical trial phase. Immunobiologicals and small molecules hold promise for future treatment regimens for chronic spontaneous urticaria.</p></section><section id="sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Study limitations</h3>
<p>Narrative reviews do not provide statistical value to the results and outcomes studied.</p></section><section id="sec6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusion</h3>
<p>A review of the pathogenesis of chronic spontaneous urticaria was conducted, contextualizing these aspects with promising drug options for its treatment, particularly immunobiologicals and small molecules.</p></section><section id="kwd0005" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Chronic urticarial, Chronic urticaria/therapy, Chronic urticaria/pathology, Immunobiologicals, Small molecules, Urticaria</p></section></section><section id="sec0005"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="par0005">Urticaria is a cutaneous inflammatory process of varying intensity in which mast cell degranulation initially leads to vasodilation, increased capillary permeability, and dermal edema (hives), accompanied or not by subcutaneous and/or submucosal edema (angioedema).<a href="#bib0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0005">1</a>, <a href="#bib0010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0010">2</a></p>
<p id="par0010">Mast cell degranulation results from several factors associated with cell receptors, physical stimuli, chemical stimuli, or those linked to immunity (innate and/or adaptive), which promote, within minutes, the release of preformed mediators (stored within intracytoplasmic granules). Newly formed mediators (lipid derivatives and cytokines/chemokines) are released minutes to hours after stimulation of these cells (<a href="#fig0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a> and <a href="#tbl0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, <a href="#tbl0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, <a href="#tbl0015" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>).<a href="#bib0010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0010"><sup>2</sup></a> In general, infections/infestations, foods, and medications are the main causes of acute urticaria.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0005" title="Figure 1"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12539477_gr1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/617e/12539477/50084c4feec8/gr1.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0005" height="606" width="742" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Mast cells and their receptors that stimulate activation, degranulation via IgE-dependent and non-IgE-dependent pathways, synthesis of newly formed mediators, and receptors that suppress mast cell activation. Top left, a still-intact mast cell in the non-urticaria skin of a patient with hives, under transmission electron microscopy (×10,000 magnification). T, Tryptase; C, Chymase.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tbl0005"><h3 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h3>
<div class="caption p"><p>Main receptors, ligands and mediators that promote mast cell activation.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Receptors and Type of Mediators</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Name</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Functions/Actions</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">Immunoglobulin superfamily receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">FcɛRI</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Expressed on mast cells and basophils as a tetramer composed of subunits (αβγ2), the α subunit responsible for binding the FcɛRI receptor to IgE, the β subunit for regulating receptor expression and signaling, and the γ subunit responsible for signal transduction. FcɛRI circulates free in the blood, either in a soluble form (sFcɛRI) or bound to IgE. IgE binds to FcɛRI with high affinity (Kd ∼10-9 to 10-10), and the dissociation half-life of IgE from FcɛRI is very slow, on the order of weeks. Due to IgE's high affinity for the receptor and slow dissociation, there is long-lasting sensitization of mast cells exposed to specific antigens.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">FcγRIII</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">When activated by IgG1, in mice, it promotes anaphylaxis</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="12" colspan="1">G protein-coupled receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Mas-related G protein-coupled receptor X2 (MRGPRX2)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Expressed predominantly in mast cells of the skin, peripheral neurons, basophils, and eosinophils. Activated by eosinophil major basic protein (MBP) and eosinophil cationic protein, fluoroquinolone antibiotics, opioids, neuromuscular blockers, human β-defensin 2 (hBD2), cathelicidin (LL-37), neuropeptides (such as substance P), Calcitonin Gene-Related Peptide (CGRP), Vasoactive Intestinal Substance (VIP), Staphylococcus aureus δ-toxin, hormone receptor modulators, phenothiazines, corticostatin-14, icatibant, cetrorelix, leuprolide, octreotide, sermorelin, atracurium, tubocurarine, rocuronium, and herbal medicines.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Histamine receptors H1, H2 and H4 (H1R, H2R and H4R)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">The binding of histamine to its H1R on mast cells stimulates the release of more histamine and other mediators, increasing the expression of cell adhesion molecules and chemotaxis of eosinophils and neutrophils, in addition to increasing antigen presentation capacity and costimulatory activity in B cells, and suppressing IgE production. Histamine binding to the H2R inhibits mast cell chemotaxis, as well as that of eosinophils and neutrophils, suppressing Th2 responses. Stimulation of the H4R leads to increased intracellular calcium influx, mast cell degranulation, and the release of cytokines, cysteinyl leukotrienes, and leukotriene B4 (LTB4). It is also relevant to the histaminergic pathways of cutaneous sensory nerve endings that conduct pruritus.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Adenosine receptor (A3R)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Adenosine receptors differ in the type of G protein they recruit, their effect on adenylyl cyclase (AC) activity, and the downstream signaling pathway they trigger. Adenosine can both increase and inhibit mast cell degranulation, indicating that its effects on these receptors are controversial and still need to be clarified. Depending on the study model, A1, A2b, and A3 receptors demonstrate anti- or pro-inflammatory activity.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Cystil-Leukotriene Receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Cysteinyl leukotrienes (CysLTs), released from mast cells, are important mediators of allergy. Type 1 receptors for CysLTs (CysLT1Rs) are involved in accelerating IgE-mediated MC activation.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">Protease-activated receptor (PAR) types 2 (PAR2) and 4 (PAR4)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PAR2: can be activated by proteases (chymase, tryptase)</td>
</tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PAR4: activated by thrombin.</td></tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Corticotropin-releasing factor (CRF) receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CRF1 receptor: induces the release of VEGF. They are co-receptors of VEGFR-2.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Calcium sensor (CaS)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Increases mast cell activity.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Serotonin receptor (5-HTR)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Amplifies mast cell activation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Acetylcholine receptor (Muscarinic M)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its binding to acetylcholine evokes mast cell degranulation.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Chemokine receptors: CCR 1-3 and CXCR1-4</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">The CCR3 receptor has high affinity for eotaxin-1/CCL-11, eotaxin-2/CCL-24, and eotaxin-3/CCl-26 and mediates mast cell migration</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Endothelin receptor (ET<sub>A,B</sub>)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Via this receptor, endothelin-1 determines mast cell degranulation and can induce the production of TNF-α and IL-6.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">Activated complement receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">C3aR</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Activates mast cells, amplifying degranulation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">C5aR</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Activates mast cells, amplifying degranulation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Maturation, differentiation, and activation receptor (type II receptor of tyrosine kinase)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">c-Kit (CD117)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its ligand targets Stem Cell Factor (SCF), for which it has high affinity (Kd −200 pM), causing receptor dimerization and phosphorylation of tyrosine residues. It is expressed in mast cells, hematopoietic and non-hematopoietic cells (such as melanocytes and interstitial cells of Cajal), and some tumors. In mast cells, it is expressed from their progenitors until their differentiation and maturation, and their development and survival depend on its activation by SCF, mediated intracellularly by the PI3K pathway, causing degranulation in response to allergens and cytokine production.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Neuropilin Receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Receptors NRP 1 and 2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Relevant in angiogenesis</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Tyrosine kinase receptors for angiopoietin</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Tie 1 and Tie 2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its ligand is angiopoietin, which, together with VEGFs, is important for the proliferation, migration and survival of endothelial cells, including the formation of lymphatic vessels.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Neurokinin Receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">NK1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">This receptor is expressed when the mast cell is stimulated by IL4 or SCF.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Pattern Recognition Molecular Receptors (PAMPs and DAMPs)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">TLR 1,2, 3,4, 5, 6,7, 8,9 e 10</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recognition of bacteria and fungi in particular, as well as peptides such as Gram-negative lipopolysaccharides (LPS), which can reach the bloodstream due to dysbiosis of the intestinal microbiome. Viral receptors (TLRs 3, 5, 7, and 9) are intracytoplasmic.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Major histocompatibility antigen</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">MHCII</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">HLA-DR</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Sphingosine-1phosphate receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">S1PR<sub>2</sub>
</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Upregulates both allergen-induced degranulation and chemokine secretion</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Glycosylphosphatidylinositol (GPI)-anchored cell surface receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CD48</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Expressed on mast cells, eosinophils, and nearly all hematopoietic cells, including basophils, it is regulated by bacterial and viral products and immune-associated proteins. It is important as a costimulatory molecule in lymphocyte activation, facilitating cell adhesion and innate responses to bacteria (such as S. aureus and its exotoxins). Its soluble form (sCD48) is elevated in lymphoproliferative diseases, Sjögren's syndrome, and asthma compared to healthy individuals.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="7" colspan="1">Receptors of Interleukins and Other Cytokines</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-33</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">ST2 receptor: its ligand, IL-33, is a member of the IL-1 superfamily, initiating and amplifying the responses of type (2) innate lymphoid cells by stimulating the synthesis of IL-5 and IL-13 by these cells. IL-33 is also synthesized by mast cells after IgE-mediated activation and acts autocrinely on the mast cells themselves, stimulating them. IL-33 produced by the bronchial epithelium stimulates mast cells to produce IL-1β and IL-6, inducing the differentiation of Th0 lymphocytes into the Th17 phenotype after challenge with ovalbumin (OVA).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-4</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-4 Rα (type I) and IL-4Rα coupled to IL-13Rα2 (type II): when IL-4 binds to these receptors, it decreases mast cell proliferation, increases the expression of ICAM-1 (Intercellular Adhesion Molecule-1) and reduces the expression of c-Kit, but in synergy with SCF, IL-4 promotes mast cell proliferation and directs the production of cytokines in the IgE-dependent pattern.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-5</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-5Rα: activates mast cells, but is crucial for stimulating eosinophils, prolonging their survival, activation, adhesion to endothelial cells, differentiation and maturation, in addition to promoting the interaction between mast cells and eosinophils</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-6</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL6R: activated by IL-6, also synthesized by mast cells and innate immunity</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) types 1 and 2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">VEGF1R and VEGF2R: Mast cells can express receptors for VEGF, which they produce.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Interferon gamma receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">INFγ-Rα: INFgamma can determine mast cell apoptosis.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Thymic Stromal Lymphopoietin (TSLP)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">TSLPR: is a heterodimer composed of the α-chain and α-chain of IL-7R, and is the target of TSLP, produced primarily by epithelial and endothelial cells, which causes differentiation and proliferation of mast cells. It also induces the production of chemokines and the synthesis of Th2-type cytokines.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Prostaglandin D2 Receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CRTH2 or DP2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PGD2 is the main metabolite derived from arachidonic acid, released by IgE-activated mast cells. CRTH2 or DP2 is present intracellularly in mast cells and on the membranes of other cells such as LTh2, eosinophils, basophil macrophages, and dendritic cells. By binding to the PGD2 receptor, it mediates cell chemotaxis and promotes degranulation of mast cells, basophils, and eosinophils.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="3" colspan="1">Costimulatory molecules</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CD40L (CD154)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Transmembrane glycoprotein that binds CD40 on other immune cells, such as CD4+ T cells and platelets. The interaction between CD40L and CD40 on B cells is relevant for immunoglobulin class switching and memory B cell generation. Blocking the CD40L-CD40 interaction reduces the generation of regulatory T cells (Tregs). Activation of CD40 allows the expression of costimulatory molecules such as CD80 and CD86.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">OX40L (CD134L)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Involved in interaction with antigen-presenting cells (macrophages). Interacts with OX40 (CD134) of T lymphocytes, including promoting Th17-dependent inflammation, together with TNF-α and IL-6.</td>
</tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">The interaction between OX40L and its ligand OX40 on T cells induces T cell expansion and proliferation and decreases the immunosuppressive effect of regulatory T cells (Treg)</td></tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Angiotensin-converting enzyme type 2 receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">ACE2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Studies demonstrate its presence in mast cells of the respiratory tree.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tbl0010"><h3 class="obj_head">Table 2.</h3>
<div class="caption p"><p>Main receptors, ligands and mediators that suppress mast cell activation.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Receptors and Type of Mediators</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Name</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Functions/Actions</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">Cannabinoids</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CB1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Under the action of endocannabinoids (anandamide and 2-arachidonoylglycerol), it promotes regulatory actions. In mast cells, it has anti-inflammatory effects, with CB1 suppressing mast cell degranulation by increasing cytosolic cAMP levels.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CB2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Under the action of endocannabinoids (anandamide and 2-arachidonoylglycerol), it promotes regulatory actions. In mast cells, it has anti-inflammatory effects by suppressing the release of pro-inflammatory mediators.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Sphingosine-1phosphate receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">S1PR<sub>1</sub>
</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation by agonists decreases allergic inflammation in the airways and reduces the accumulation of eosinophils and T cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Immunoglobulins</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">FcγRIIb (low-affinity receptor for IgG type b) (expressed only in mice, but not in human mast cells)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">In murine mast cells, FcγRIIb can cross-link adjacent FcɛRI on the cell membrane with the same multivalent ligand and form an immune complex and inhibit FcɛRI-dependent mast cell activation, preventing degranulation and synthesis of newly formed mediators.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CD300 Family</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CD300a</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its ligands are phosphatidylserine and phosphatidylethanolamine (expressed in the early stages of apoptosis/cell death) as membrane signals for phagocytosis. In the intracellular space, its action activates the SHP-1 protein, dephosphorylates SYK, and decreases intracellular calcium influx. CD300a expression on mast cells can be inhibited by eosinophil major basic protein (MBP) and eosinophil-derived neurotoxins.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="3" colspan="1">Siglecs Family (sialic acid-binding immunoglobulin-like lectins)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Siglec-6</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation prevents mast cell activation.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Siglec-7</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation results in inhibition of mediator release via FcɛRI-dependent pathway, by cross-linking with adjacent FcɛRI on the cell membrane.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Siglec-8</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Stimulated by IL-5, IL-33, or GM-CSF, mast cell apoptosis is induced. Activation of mast cell mediators results in FcɛRI-dependent inhibition of mast cell mediator release.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Leukocyte Ig-like receptor (LIR)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibitory receptors LIR-1, LIR-2 and LIR-3</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">LIR-1 is expressed in mature mast cells and LIR2 and LIR-3 in mast cell progenitors. They promote inhibition of mast cell activity.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Membrane glycoprotein receptor belonging to the SIRP family</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">SIRP-α</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation inhibits FcɛRI-mediated mast cell activation in mice</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CD200R</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CD200R</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation inhibits FcɛRI-mediated mast cell activation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">c-lectin mast cell function-associated antigen (a variant of lectin-like type II transmembranal glycoprotein)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">MAFA</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibits degranulation and cytokine release by IgE-activated mast cells in rat mucosa</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Platelet-Endothelial Cell Adhesion Molecule-1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PECAM-1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation inhibits FcɛRI-mediated mast cell activation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Leukocyte-associated I-like receptor (LAIR)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">LAIR-1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Its activation results in the reduction of preformed mediators and the release of cytokines. Its ligand is collagen.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Prostanoid-E Receptors</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">EP2 e EP4</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Prostaglandin E2 (PGE2) binds to the EP2 receptor and inhibits mast cell degranulation, cytokine transcription, and eicosanoid production via stimulation of FcɛRI. Binding to EP4 inhibits mast cell activation. One of the mechanisms of urticaria caused by nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs is the decrease in PGE2 synthesis and loss of its mast cell-silencing mechanisms.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Programmed death receptor</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PDL1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Induces mast cell apoptosis</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tbl0015"><h3 class="obj_head">Table 3.</h3>
<div class="caption p"><p>Major preformed and newly formed receptors, ligands and mediators.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>
<th colspan="2" align="left" rowspan="1">Receptors and Type of Mediators</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Name</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Functions/Actions</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="18" colspan="1">Preformed (generally stored in granules of 0.2 to 0.8 μm in diameter, of skin mast cells. TC mast cells, chymase-tryptase positive)</td>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">Amines</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Histamine</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Vasodilation, expression of VCAM in the vascular endothelium activating endothelial cells, increased capillary permeability, stimulation of pruritus</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Polyamines</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Vasodilation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="3" colspan="1">Proteoglycans</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Heparin</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Anticoagulation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Chondroitin sulfate</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibits the activation of other connective tissue mast cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Serglycin</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Proteoglycan to which heparin binds and whose function is to maintain the homeostasis of mast cell granules to be secreted.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="5" colspan="1">Proteases</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Tryptases (expressed only in mast cells)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Tryptase-α, Tryptase-βI Tryptase-βII, Tryptase-βIII, Tryptase-γ, Tryptase-δ: Increased capillary permeability, stimulation of pruritus via PAR2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Chymase-1 (expressed only in skin mast cells)</td>
<td align="left" rowspan="4" colspan="1">Protease stored in skin mast cell granules, promoting inflammation and tissue destruction</td>
</tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Cathepsin G</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Carboxypeptidase A</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Granzyme B 3</td></tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="3" colspan="1">Lysosomal enzymes</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">β-glucoronidase</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Glycosidase that participates in the catabolism of mucopolysaccharides</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">β-Hexosaminidase</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Involved in hydrolysis mechanisms.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Arylsulfatase</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Catalyzes reactions of sulfonated hexoses.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="5" colspan="1">Cytokines</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">TNF-α</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Stimulates TH1 polarization</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">bFGF</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Stimulation of fibroblasts in the regeneration of the intercellular matrix</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-4</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Stimulates type 2 inflammation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">SCF</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Maturation, differentiation and increased survival of mast cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">VEGFb</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Increased capillary permeability and angiogenesis</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="45" colspan="1">Neoformed (synthesized minutes to hours after mast cell stimulation)</td>
<td align="left" rowspan="11" colspan="1">Lipids</td>
<td align="left" rowspan="4" colspan="1">Prostaglandin D2 (PGD2)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Vasodilation and increased capillary permeability</td>
</tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment and activation of Th2 lymphocytes</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Chemotaxis and activation of basophils and eosinophils</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Migration of type 2 innate immune lymphoid cells (ILC2)</td></tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="2" colspan="1">Leukotriene B4 (LTB4)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Chemotaxis of neutrophils, mast cells, and dendritic cells</td>
</tr>
<tr><td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of CD8+ lymphocytes</td></tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Leukotriene C4 (LTC4)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Eosinophil migration</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Leukotriene D4 (LTD4)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Migration of eosinophils and neutrophils</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Leukotriene E4 (LTD4)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Migration of eosinophils and ILC2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Prostaglandin E2 (PGE2)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Stabilization of mast cells and their decreased activation and migration of dendritic cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Platelet activating factor (PAF)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Vasodilation, increased capillary permeability, increases adhesion of blood cells to the vascular endothelium and promotes migration to the extravascular space</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="20" colspan="1">Cytokines</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Interleukin-1 (IL-1) (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of eosinophils, neutrophils, migration of dendritic cells, activation of the inflammasome; IL-1β determines plasma extravasation from vessels</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Interferon type I (INF-α and INF-β) and II (INF-γ and has decreased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Secreted in viral infections such as respiratory syncytial virus. It leads to the aggregation of lymphocytes, eosinophils, mast cells, macrophages, and neutrophils.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-2 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Proliferation of effector T lymphocytes and B lymphocytes; development of Treg cells; growth factor for B lymphocytes and stimulation of antibody synthesis</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-3 (increased expression in CSU lesions)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Activation of eosinophils and basophils; enhancement of FcεRI expression in basophils and stimulation of their longer survival</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-4 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Activation of T lymphocytes and basophils; increased humoral immunity; recruitment of eosinophils; differentiation from Th0 to Th2 with induction of type 2 inflammatory response and monocytes; differentiation into M2 macrophages; factor of increased survival for T and B lymphocytes; promotes immunoglobulin class switching, determining the production of IgE and IgG1 by B lymphocytes.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-5 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Increase in the number of eosinophils due to increased adhesion and chemotaxis capacity to the skin.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-6 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Differentiation of B lymphocytes and subsequent production of IgG, IgM and IgA; increased mast cell proliferation, maturation and reactivity (priming)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-8 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Acts as a chemoattractant for T lymphocytes, basophils, eosinophils, NK cells, and neutrophils</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-9 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Acts as a growth factor for mast cells and T lymphocytes; inhibits Th1-type cytokines and promotes the proliferation of CD8+ lymphocytes and mast cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-10 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibits Th1 and Tc1 cell function; activates B lymphocytes and induces autoantibody production by B cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-12</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of CD8+ T lymphocytes; induction of Th1 lymphocyte responses</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-13 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Activation of mast cells and eosinophils; increased eosinophil survival</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-16</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">T lymphocyte recruitment and dendritic cell migration</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-17A (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Induction of pro-inflammatory cytokines and chemokines, with recruitment of neutrophils</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-18</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Dendritic cell migration</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-25 (increased expression in CSU lesions)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Induction of Th2 responses and inhibition of Th1 and Th17 responses; induction of the production of IgE and IgG1, IL-4, IL-5, IL-9 and IL-13</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-31 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Induction of IL-6 and IL-8, CXCL1, CXCL8, CCL2 and CCL8 in eosinophils</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">IL-33 (increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Increases allergic stimulation of mast cells and basophils; induces IL-31 production in mast cells; increases integrin expression in basophils and eosinophils, leading them to the skin; promotes mast cell maturation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">TNF-α ( “de novo” synthesis: increased serum expression in CSU)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Promotes activation of endothelial cells, proliferation of effector T lymphocytes, increases the expression of ICAM-1 in eosinophils allowing them to migrate to the skin; activation of monocytes and macrophages, recruitment of neutrophils and proliferation of T lymphocytes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">GM-CSF</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Determines migration of dendritic cells to lymph nodes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" rowspan="14" colspan="1">Chemokines</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of T cells and monocytes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of T cells, monocytes, and neutrophil migration</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL3</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of T cells and monocytes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL4</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of T cells and monocytes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL5</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of CD8+ T cells, monocytes, basophils, eosinophils, NK cells and dendritic cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL7</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of T cells: monocytes, basophils, eosinophils, NK cells, immature dendritic cells, and hematopoietic progenitor cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL18</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of naive T cells, CD4+ and CD8+ T lymphocytes, memory T lymphocytes, B lymphocytes, and immature dendritic cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CCL20</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of B lymphocytes, memory effector T lymphocytes, recruitment of CD11b+ dendritic cells</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CXCL1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Neutrophil recruitment</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CXCL2</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of neutrophils, basophils, and eosinophils</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CXCL3</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Neutrophil recruitment</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CXCL8</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of neutrophils and CD4+ T lymphocytes</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">XCL1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Dendritic cell migration and antigen cross-presentation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CXCCL1</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Recruitment of monocytes and T lymphocytes and increased survival</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl0015/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="par0015">The main mediator responsible for the initial events of urticaria is histamine, which binds to H1 and H2 receptors on the vascular endothelium, determining the effects of vasodilation, increased capillary permeability (in conjunction with other mediators released by mast cells), and pruritus, its cardinal symptom, initially caused by stimulation of histaminergic cutaneous sensory pathways (via H1 and H4 receptors) and the action of tryptase.<a href="#bib0010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0010"><sup>2</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0020">Hives are transitory, generally lasting in the same location for no more than 24 hours, while angioedema is more long-lasting (varying between 48 and 96 hours).<a href="#bib0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0005"><sup>1</sup></a> Urticaria is classified according to its temporal evolution as acute (&lt;6 weeks) or chronic (&gt;6 weeks). Although hives are common in Chronic Urticaria (CU), 43% to 59% of patients also present with angioedema; however, approximately 10% present with angioedema alone (mast cell angioedema).<a href="#bib0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0005"><sup>1</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0025">Over time, in addition to pre-synthesized substances (histamine, tryptase, and other proteases) released by damaged mast cell granules, “de novo” synthesized mediators, such as lipid derivatives (prostaglandins and leukotrienes) and cytokines/chemokines, are released by mast cells, activating the cutaneous vascular endothelium (expression of cell adhesion molecules such as PECAM-1/CD31, E-selectin, P-selectin, ICAM-1, VEGF expression, and tissue factor production), and leading to the chemotaxis of other blood cells to the skin, such as basophils, lymphocytes, eosinophils, and neutrophils. These recruited cells, like a feedback loop, determine the establishment of chronic and intermittent inflammatory responses in distinct areas of the skin, which are recurrent and of varying intensity. Other mediators, besides histamine, play a key role in the chronicity of the disease.<a href="#bib0010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0010"><sup>2</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0030">Conceptually, Chronic Urticaria (CU) is classified as induced: related to identifiable and generally external triggering stimuli, such as cold, heat, pressure, water, solar radiation, or even cholinergic or symptomatic dermographism; or spontaneous, when the trigger is not always the same, appearing in different situations throughout the disease's history.<a href="#bib0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0005"><sup>1</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0035">This article aims to present a review of the pathogenesis of Chronic Spontaneous Urticaria (CSU), as well as to support the rationale for its current and developing therapies.</p></section><section id="sec0010"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h2>
<p id="par0040">This narrative review addressed the literature from January 1977 to October 2024, in PubMed/Medline, using the terms (<em>idiopathic</em> OR <em>spontaneous</em> = 4.169) AND <em>urticaria, prevalence</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (3.320), <em>genetic</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (2.486), <em>autoantibodies</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (766), <em>coagulation</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (757), <em>gut</em> AND <em>microbiota</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (43), <em>stress</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (482)<em>, treatment</em> AND <em>urticaria</em> (13.709), totaling 25.732 articles. The authors selected relevant articles on these topics that have provided advances in these areas. Priority was given to articles and records in Clinical Trials (ClinicalTrials.gov) addressing aspects of the pathogenesis of chronic spontaneous urticaria, its currently recommended treatment, and the prospects for new medications such as small molecules or biological agents.</p></section><section id="sec0015"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Prevalence and Natural History of Urticaria</h2>
<p id="par0045">Different population groups have variable prevalence rates for CU. Latin America has the highest prevalence, 1.5% (95% CI 0.0–6.0), followed by Asia, 1.4% (95% CI 0.5–2.9), Europe, 0.5% (95% CI 0.2–1.0), and North America, 0.1% (95% CI 0.1–1.0). Women are more affected by CU than adult men, while no gender differences were observed in children.<a href="#bib0015" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0015"><sup>3</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0050">In Brazil, a retrospective study estimated a 1.7% prevalence of CU among dermatological patients.<a href="#bib0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0020"><sup>4</sup></a> Another study, which analyzed retrospective data from 2011, 2012, and 2015, from a national health and well-being survey with 36,000 respondents, resulted in a 0.41% prevalence of CU.<a href="#bib0025" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0025"><sup>5</sup></a> In Brazil, two studies demonstrated a higher incidence of CU in adults among women, with estimates ranging from 80%<a href="#bib0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0020"><sup>4</sup></a> to 86%.<a href="#bib0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0030"><sup>6</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0055">There is little published data on the natural history of urticaria, and some of it has methodological limitations, most focusing on the duration of CSU.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a> In general, the frequency of progression of acute urticaria (AU) cases to CU is not adequately explored, and some studies are conducted in hospital settings rather than population-based.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0060">In an evaluation of data from the Korean national healthcare system between 2002 and 2013, with a population sample of 1,025,340 individuals, a total of 4.8% were identified with urticaria, which determined its annual incidence rate ranging from 45.7 to 82.5 per 100,000 person-years.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a> In this study, among all patients with urticaria; 6.1% were identified with progression to CU. Among these; 46.6% already had CU when they were first diagnosed with urticaria. The remainder had AU at their first diagnosis, but they subsequently developed CU when the disease recurred after a median of 614 days.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a> Over the five-year and ten-year periods, the cumulative incidence rates of CU among all patients with urticaria ranged between 6.3% and 7.8%, respectively, and approximately 50% of patients with CU experienced remission after 11 months of disease, with CU remission rates ranging from 52.6% in the first year of disease, 78% in the third year, and 89% at 5 years.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0065">Higher financial income was associated with higher CU remission rates.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a> Furthermore, the study demonstrated that several other clinical factors interfered with the progression to CU, conferring a higher risk: age ≥ 10 years, male gender, urban residence, and autoimmune thyroid disease. Women were more affected by AU than men in the age range between 20–44 years and 45–64 years, which may be linked to estrogen, which stimulates humoral immunity.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035"><sup>7</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0070">Regarding the average duration of CSU, in patients aged ≥12 years, it is estimated to last approximately five years, although it can persist for longer periods in severe cases.<a href="#bib0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0040"><sup>8</sup></a> In a review that included eight observational studies and two reviews, aimed at determining the duration of the CSU course and disease remission rates, the average age ranged from 34 to 68 years, and the proportion of women ranged from 61% to 80%.<a href="#bib0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0040"><sup>8</sup></a> The proportion of patients who achieved CSU remission within the first year of disease ranged from 21% to 47%, while remission rates reported in the fifth year of disease were between 34% and 45%.<a href="#bib0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0040"><sup>8</sup></a> Based on a four-week consecutive CSU remission, cumulative remission rates ranged from 9% to 38% in the first year, from 29% to 71% in the fifth year of disease, and from 52% to 93% in the twentieth year of CSU.<a href="#bib0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0040"><sup>8</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0075">Chronic pruritus without hives or angioedema occurred in 12.2% of patients. There was a greater likelihood of CSU persisting in patients diagnosed with hypothyroidism (HR = 0.43; 95% CI 0.29–0.60) and with each point increase in the UAS7 severity score (HR = 0.93; 95% CI 0.92–0.95).<a href="#bib0045" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0045"><sup>9</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0080">Thus, regarding the natural course of the disease, urticaria is a condition that will affect approximately 10% to 20% of the world's population at some point in their lives. There is a consensus that acute spontaneous urticaria is common; however, it is often associated with upper respiratory tract infections. Most of these infections are self-limited, and less than 10% progress to CU, although some studies indicate higher rates.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035">7</a>, <a href="#bib0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0050">10</a>, <a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a> CU, in turn, persists for periods of &gt; 1 year in most patients, significantly impairing quality of life, causing psychiatric and systemic comorbidities, and incurring high costs to the healthcare system.<a href="#bib0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0035">7</a>, <a href="#bib0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0040">8</a>, <a href="#bib0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0050">10</a>, <a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a></p></section><section id="sec0020"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Genetic and Epigenetic Basis</h2>
<p id="par0085">CU is a multifactorial disease, in which the hypersensitivity reaction is based on a genetic and epigenetic basis that inflicts susceptibility.</p>
<p id="par0090">Regarding histocompatibility antigens, HLA-Bw35 has been associated with CU and other endocrine diseases in the Japanese population.<a href="#bib0060" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0060"><sup>12</sup></a> CD4+ T lymphocytes in the infiltrate, as well as vascular endothelium, sweat glands, and nerves, express HLA-DR.<a href="#bib0065" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0065">13</a>, <a href="#bib0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0070">14</a>, <a href="#bib0075" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0075">15</a></p>
<p id="par0095">In Caucasian patients with CSU, a strong association with HLA-DRB1*04 (HLA-DR4) was found in patients who manifested histamine-releasing activity in their serum, demonstrating that, despite being heterogeneous, CU has an autoimmune pathogenesis in a subgroup of patients.<a href="#bib0080" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0080"><sup>16</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0100">In a sample of Chinese individuals, CSU patients had lower methylation in 439 genes, with 86.5% of these genes hypomethylated, especially on chromosome 6, suggesting an autoimmune behavior similar to diseases such as rheumatoid arthritis, multiple sclerosis, and lupus. The study also identified that the sphingolipid pathway, particularly sphingosine-1-phosphate (S1p), may be involved in CSU, as hypomethylation increases its production, influencing inflammation and the immune response. Indeed, the prevalence of autoimmune diseases in CSU patients is high, and many have specific antibodies and a family history of autoimmunity.<a href="#bib0085" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0085">17</a>, <a href="#bib0090" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0090">18</a></p>
<p id="par0105">Under homeostatic conditions, S1p levels are low in tissues, but mast cells can release S1p in response to stimuli, which increases capillary permeability and recruits immune cells, contributing to allergic inflammation. S1p, through the s1p1 receptor, promotes Th17 T-cell differentiation and can polarize lymphocytes toward the Th2 immunophenotype, in addition to suppressing the differentiation of thymic and extrathymic T-regulatory (LTreg) cells, leading to chronic inflammation.<a href="#bib0095" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0095"><sup>19</sup></a> The autocrine effect of s1p on sip2 receptors on mast cells themselves leads to degranulation.<a href="#bib0095" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0095"><sup>19</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0110">In a GWAS study, CSU was associated with SNPs in the genes TDGF1, HLA-G, PTPN22, LILRA3, and IGHG1/IGHG2, which explain 8.07% of CSU heritability at the estimated disease prevalence point of 0.5%–1% in the general population. The first three are associated with CSU autoimmune phenotypes.<a href="#bib0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0100"><sup>20</sup></a> These alterations underlie macrophage activation in the pathogenesis of CSU.<a href="#bib0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0100">20</a>, <a href="#bib0105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0105">21</a></p>
<p id="par0115">The TDGF1 gene increases the phagocytic activity of macrophages and stimulates the production of pro-inflammatory and anti-inflammatory cytokines through the NF-Kb pathway.<a href="#bib0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0100">20</a>, <a href="#bib0110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0110">22</a> The transcription factor c-Maf determines the production of IL-10 by type 2 phenotype macrophages (M2 macrophages), helping to perpetuate inflammation mediated by Th2 lymphocytes, which maintain the inflammatory process and contribute to the chronicity of urticaria.<a href="#bib0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0100">20</a>, <a href="#bib0105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0105">21</a> The HLA-G gene identified among the alleles of SNPs related to CSU encodes HLA-G, an HLA class I molecule that induces immunotolerance by suppressing the functions of NK cells, CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, and dendritic cells. Therefore, elevated levels of soluble HLA-G are observed in patients with allergic rhinitis and asthma, which correlate with serum IgE specific to allergens.<a href="#bib0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0100">20</a>, <a href="#bib0115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0115">23</a></p>
<p id="par0120">Two studies demonstrated an association between CSU and autoimmune diseases with the CRTH2<a href="#bib0120" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0120"><sup>24</sup></a> and ORAI1 genes.<a href="#bib0125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0125"><sup>25</sup></a> Other studies indicated a relationship between CSU and the HLA-DRB1*04,<a href="#bib0130" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0130"><sup>26</sup></a> HLA-DRB1*01, HLA-B44 and HLA-DRB*15 alleles,<a href="#bib0135" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0135"><sup>27</sup></a> TGF-β1,<a href="#bib0140" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0140"><sup>28</sup></a> PTPN22 (a factor that regulates the production of anti-TPO and anti-TG IgG)<a href="#bib0145" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0145"><sup>29</sup></a> and IL-2.<a href="#bib0150" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0150"><sup>30</sup></a> In particular, the presence of an association between CSU and the CRTH2 and TGF-β1 genes is related to cases associated with allergic/atopic asthma, and with HLA-DRB1*01, HLA-B44 and HLA-DRB*15 with autoimmune diseases, but not atopic disease.<a href="#bib0100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0100"><sup>20</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0125">In a study with knocked-down Human Mast Cell Line-1 (HMC-1) after incubation with thrombin to mimic the pathogenesis of CSU, Fang et al.<a href="#bib0155" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0155"><sup>31</sup></a> observed the relevance of the CCL2 (Chemokine C-C motif ligand-2) and CH25H (Cholesterol 25-Hydroxylase) genes and the tumor necrosis factor signaling pathway.</p>
<p id="par0130">In another GWAS study, two loci associated with CSU risk were identified: HLA-DQA1 and ITPKB. Inositol 1,4,5-Triphosphate Kinase B (ITPKB) is part of a family of kinases that phosphorylate inositol as a second messenger in the calcium signaling pathway.<a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160"><sup>32</sup></a> The regulation of inositol-1,4,5 is involved in neuronal signaling and immune function, including mast cell and basophil degranulation, suggesting that ITPKB may play a role in the loss of tolerance in CSU and enhance calcium-dependent secretion responses in mast cells.<a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160"><sup>32</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0135">Based on these models, stimulated primarily by exogenous factors, a small proportion of patients with acute urticaria, under the influence of endogenous factors (such as genetic and epigenetic aspects), interact within the phenotypic spectrum of CSU, where different HLAs and gene polymorphisms determine a breakdown in immune tolerance, with a predisposition to the disease and an association with other autoimmune conditions, which together determine the background of the disease pathogenesis (<a href="#fig0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0010" title="Figure 2"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12539477_gr2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/617e/12539477/680dae9a2973/gr2.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0010" height="1043" width="747" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Main epigenetic and genetic mechanisms involved in acute urticaria in its evolution to chronic spontaneous urticaria.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec0025"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Pathogenesis</h2>
<p id="par0140">The pathogenesis of CSU is centered on the activity of mast cells, whose degranulation in the skin and subcutaneous/submucosal tissues leads to the formation of hives and angioedema, and its cardinal symptom, pruritus. Therefore, CSU is considered a mast cell-dependent disease, with the participation of basophils and other cell elements that are attracted to the skin, involving various immune mechanisms such as autoimmunity, inflammation, coagulation factors, and activation of mast cell membrane receptors (<a href="#fig0015" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>, <a href="#fig0020" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>), which cause chronic inflammation.<a href="#bib0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0050">10</a>, <a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160">32</a></p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0015" title="Figure 3"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12539477_gr3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/617e/12539477/64132a779fbf/gr3.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0015" height="473" width="742" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0015/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Mast cell activation and degranulation to external antigens in IgE-mediated reactions outside the context of chronic spontaneous urticaria: IgE specific to a given allergen, binding to contiguous FcεRI receptors on the mast cell membrane, and intracellular signaling events mediated by Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK). The phenomenon culminates in mast cell degranulation and synthesis of newly formed mediators.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig0020" title="Figure 4"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12539477_gr4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/617e/12539477/a7a6fca4d770/gr4.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0020" height="927" width="742" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig0020/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(A) Type I autoimmunity mechanism (autoallergy), with the participation of IgE alloantibodies against circulating autoantigens. (B) Mechanism IIb (autoimmunity) mediated by the presence of IgG autoantibodies, possibly IgM and IgA, against the alpha subunit of the high-affinity IgE receptor (FcɛRIα) on mast cells and basophils, or against the patient's own IgE, either circulating or bound to FcɛRI receptors. In both mechanisms, mast cell degranulation and synthesis of newly formed mediators occur through the activation of specific intracellular and nuclear signaling pathways, which share the activation of Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="par0145">Several authors classify CSU as an autoimmune disease, due to genetic findings and the coexistence of other autoimmune diseases.<a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160"><sup>32</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0150">Malmros H.<a href="#bib0165" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0165"><sup>33</sup></a> in 1946 described the first finding of what is termed an “autoreactivity” phenomenon in CU patients, when he injected the own serum (autologous serum) of six CU patients, as in an intradermal test, and observed the formation of erythema and hives within 30 minutes of the injection, suggesting passive transfer of antibodies. Subsequently, in 1986, it was demonstrated that seven of 12 tests of autologous serum from patients with Chronic Idiopathic Urticaria (CIU) reinjected intradermally, produced erythema and hives at the injection site.<a href="#bib0165" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0165">33</a>, <a href="#bib0170" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0170">34</a>, <a href="#bib0175" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0175">35</a></p>
<p id="par0155">In recent decades, considerable discoveries have been revealed about the mechanisms involved in mast cell activation, with the description of new activating and inhibiting receptors expressed on their cell membrane surface.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a> Most mast cell progenitors already express the c-Kit receptor and FcɛRI on their membrane, which are essential for the survival and activity of these cells.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a> Different cytokines, chemokines and adhesion molecules, such as IL-4, IL-5, IL-6 IL-15, TNF-α, CCL-2 and vascular cell adhesion molecule (VCAM-1) released into the tissue microenvironment regulate the expansion, homing and maturation of mast cell precursors. However, the binding of Stem-Cell Factor (SCF) to the c-Kit receptor remains the most potent signal of mast cell differentiation, proliferation, and survival.<a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160"><sup>32</sup></a> Different authors have described the presence of IgG and IgM autoantibodies directed against IgE and/or its membrane receptor alpha subunit (FcɛRIα),<a href="#bib0185" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0185">37</a>, <a href="#bib0190" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0190">38</a>, <a href="#bib0195" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0195">39</a>, <a href="#bib0200" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0200">40</a> in addition to basopenia in the peripheral blood of these patients.</p>
<p id="par0160">Subsequent advances led to the understanding of the existence of two main autoimmune mechanisms that determine the so-called “autoreactivity” in patients with CSU: type I autoimmunity (or autoallergic, aaCSU), primarily mediated by IgE specific to self-antigens (endogenous peptides),<a href="#bib0205" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0205"><sup>41</sup></a> with IgE directed against serum thyroperoxidase, and type IIb autoimmunity (autoimmune CSU, aiCSU), mediated by IgG,<a href="#bib0190" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0190"><sup>38</sup></a> and to a lesser extent also by IgM and IgA, directed against IgE or the FcɛRIα receptor, expressed on mast cells and basophils.<a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160"><sup>32</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0165">Both mechanisms (endotypes) suggest the common principle that CSU is fundamentally an autoimmune disease, not influenced by exogenous agents/factors.<a href="#bib0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0210"><sup>42</sup></a> However, the observation that autoantibodies are not always detected in patients with CSU indicates that mechanisms other than autoimmunity, whether immunological or non-immunological, are involved in the pathogenesis, contributing to the understanding of different CSU phenotypes.<a href="#bib0160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0160"><sup>32</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0170">Type I/aaUCE is inherent in the existence of IgE antibodies to several currently recognized antigens such as thyroperoxidase or thyroid peroxidase (TPO), thyroglobulin (TG), tissue factor (TF), eosinophil peroxidase (EPO), double-stranded DNA (dsDNA), eosinophil cationic protein (ECP), interleukin-24 (IL-24), and IgE anti-FcɛRI.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a>, <a href="#bib0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0210">42</a>, <a href="#bib0215" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0215">43</a> The most common autoantigens are TPO and IL-24, and IgE-antiTPO and IgE-antiIL-24 demonstrate <em>in vitro</em> the activation of mast cells and/or basophils.<a href="#bib0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0210">42</a>, <a href="#bib0220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0220">44</a>, <a href="#bib0225" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0225">45</a> IgE anti-IL-24 is present in approximately 70% to 80% of patients with CSU, and its serum concentration in patients with CSU correlates with disease activity.<a href="#bib0220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0220"><sup>44</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0175">When the patient's autologous serum (autohemotherapy) was used to treat CSU, the Autologous Serum Test (ASST) was negative in 28% and 34% of patients, respectively, at weeks 9 and 21. However, this did not correlate with the overall response to treatment, as there was a reduction of slightly more than 50% in the antihistamine doses required for CSU treatment compared to the period prior to autohemotherapy.<a href="#bib0230" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0230"><sup>46</sup></a> The selective activation of mast cells in the skin by IgE autoantibodies can be explained by the occurrence of certain autoallergens, expressed almost exclusively in the skin, such as IL-24, and cross-reactivity with certain proteins, such as TPO (absent in the skin) and EPO (expressed in the skin).<a href="#bib0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0210"><sup>42</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0180">Type IIb/ai CSU occurs in the form of IgG anti-FcɛRIα in approximately 20%–50% of patients.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a>, <a href="#bib0235" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0235">47</a> These autoantibodies activate mast cells and basophils, for example via IgE and FcɛRI, but are present in 8% of patients with CSU when strict criteria are applied, which include the presence of triple positivity in the following tests: ASST, immunoassay for IgG autoantibodies, and Basophil Activation Test (BAT).<a href="#bib0235" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0235"><sup>47</sup></a> Type IIb autoimmune CSU is characterized by the presence of high disease activity (elevated UAS7), concomitant with other autoimmune diseases (such as Hashimoto's thyroiditis), low serum levels of total IgE, high levels of IgG anti-thyroid peroxidase (TPO) antibodies, basopenia (&lt; 10 cells/μL) and eosinopenia (&lt; 50 cells/μL),<a href="#bib0240" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0240"><sup>48</sup></a> in the peripheral blood and poor therapeutic response to antihistamines, poor response or partial response to omalizumab, in addition to a good therapeutic response to cyclosporine.<a href="#bib0235" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0235"><sup>47</sup></a> In mechanism IIb, the presence of IgG (subtypes IgG1 and IgG3) was demonstrated in 24% of the patients evaluated, and more than half of the patients had IgM (60%) or IgA (5%) against the FcɛRI receptor (most common), when these antibodies were tested by the ELISA method and against IgE.<a href="#bib0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0210">42</a>, <a href="#bib0245" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0245">49</a> In clinical practice, it is recommended to evaluate the relationship between serum levels of anti-thyroid peroxidase IgG/total serum IgE, which in these patients can result in values above 15, as an assessment of the existence of aiUCE (type IIb), since serum levels of total IgE &lt;40 IU/mL are frequently observed.<a href="#bib0250" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0250"><sup>50</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0185">In fact, many patients with CSU show the coexistence of auto-IgE and IgG autoantibodies (coexistence of type I and type IIb mechanisms), which indicates an overlap between these two endotypes.<a href="#bib0215" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0215">43</a>, <a href="#bib0255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0255">51</a> In Brazil, the authors observe 38% of patients with type I mechanism endotypes, 51% with concomitant type I and IIb mechanism endotypes, 9% with IIb endotypes, and 2% of patients who did not meet criteria for any endotype.<a href="#bib0255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0255"><sup>51</sup></a> Thus, overlapping autoreactivity mechanisms in CSU types I and IIb, with IgG and IgE anti-TPO, can coexist in the same patient. However, patients with aiUCE with or without coexisting aaUCE are generally women, with higher levels of antiperoxidase autoantibodies (both IgG and IgE anti-TPO), and demonstrate a greater negative impact on quality of life, while patients with aaUCE without association with aiUCE are younger individuals.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180">36</a>, <a href="#bib0260" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0260">52</a> However, considering different studies, less than 35% of patients with CSU will not have any autoantibodies (non-autoimmune endotype).<a href="#bib0255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0255">51</a>, <a href="#bib0260" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0260">52</a></p>
<p id="par0190">In addition to autoantibodies, another factor involved in the amplification of mast cell degranulation is the activation of the complement system through the alternative pathway. This is particularly relevant in the mechanism of type IIb CSU, where after activation of FcɛRI on the mast cell membrane by IgG-anti-FcɛRI, C5a (anaphylatoxin) is generated and this acts as a ligand for the C5aR receptor expressed only on cutaneous mast cells, and exacerbates mast cell degranulation, worsening and amplifying CSU, which makes the skin the target organ in CSU due to the unique expression of C5aR, sensitizing it to inflammatory and autoimmune processes.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0195">Understanding the pathogenesis of CSU has allowed us to clarify the action of therapeutic agents, such as the monoclonal antibody omalizumab, directed against human IgE, sequestering it and suppressing the expression of its membrane receptors (FcɛRI) on mast cells and basophils, so that there is a decrease in the density of antigen to which the anti-FcɛRI IgG is directed, with a consequent decrease in the signaling of mast cell and basophil degranulation.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a> Consequently, cross-linking between two contiguous FcɛRI receptors on the cell membrane, a necessary step for degranulation, is reduced.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a> Furthermore, as complement activation via the alternative pathway, which is caused by the aggregation of IgG1 and IgG3 to the FcɛRI antigen, responsible for complement activation, with the use of omalizumab, the expression of FcɛRI on the cell membrane of mast cells was reduced. This reduces the interaction between IgG-anti-FcɛRI and the receptor antigen, consequently decreasing the generation of C5a and its binding to the receptors C5aR on the membrane of cutaneous mast cells, silencing the signals that potentiate the activation of these cells, CSU protagonists.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0200">In addition to the FcɛRI receptor, mast cells can be activated by other activating receptors on their surface, including the Mas-related G protein-coupled receptor X2 (MRGPRX2), the C5Ra receptor, protease activated receptors (PAR1 and PAR2, which have trypsin, tryptase, thrombin, and the FVIIa/FXa/TF complex as ligands), a molecule homologous to chemoattraction receptors expressed on Th2 cells (CRTh2), and cytokine receptors (ST2, for IL-33; IL-4Rα receptor for IL-4, among others such as IL-5, IL-6, IL-15, IL-25, thymic stromal lymphopoietin [TSLP], and TNF-α), and the c-Kit receptor.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0205">The diversity of mast cell receptors (FcɛRI, MRGPRX2, PAR1 and PAR2, CRTh2, c-Kit [CD117], and receptors for various cytokines and histamine H1 and H4) makes this cell capable of being subjected to a stimulus that influences an underlying response (priming).<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055"><sup>11</sup></a> Activation of FcɛRI involves several intracytoplasmic signaling proteins, such as LYN, splenic tyrosine kinase (SYK), and Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK), which promote subsequent phosphorylation signals in other proteins and induce mast cell activation and degranulation.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055"><sup>11</sup></a> Thus, the first step of FcɛRI-mediated signaling is the phosphorylation of the FcɛRI beta chain (FcɛRIβ-chain) and gamma chain (FcɛRIγ-chain) by the LYN protein, followed by the activation of SYK and BTK.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055"><sup>11</sup></a> Thus, cytosolic BTK is the central positive regulator of mast cell activation and cytokine production in FcɛRI-mediated stimuli.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055"><sup>11</sup></a> In addition to its role in mast cell activation, BTK is required for the B Cell Receptor (BCR) signaling pathway and subsequent antibody synthesis.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055"><sup>11</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0210">In addition to the membrane receptors that activate mast cells, these cells have receptors that inhibit their function, such as CD200R, CD300a, FcγRIIb, and sialic acid-binding immunoglobulin-like lectin 8 (Siglec 8), which can block their activation and interaction with other ligands.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055"><sup>11</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0215">Of growing interest is the MRGPRX2 receptor, a G-protein-coupled receptor with seven transmembrane domains, highly expressed in mast cells of patients with CSU and overregulated in severe forms of the disease, participating in degranulation mechanisms called pseudoallergic/neurogenic.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a> Its activation occurs by several mediators, including compound 48/80, eosinophil-derived mediators (major basic protein, MBP, and EPO), neuropeptides (such as substance P, vasoactive intestinal peptide, VIP), innate host defense peptides (catechecidin), small molecule drugs (nicotine antagonists and neuromuscular blocking drugs), opioids, antibiotics (vancomycin, ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin, and moxifloxacin), and iodinated contrast media, all of which are involved in the development and exacerbation of CSU. After activation of MRGPRX2, there is an increase in calcium in the CM cytosol, mediating Ca2+ channels, Gai, Gaq, extracellular signal regulated by kinase, PI3K/AKT, and phosphoinositide phospholipase Cγ with subsequent degranulation and further cytokine release.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a> Communication between eosinophils and mast cells, independent of IgE-related mechanisms, can occur in CSU due to MBP and EPO binding to MRGPRX2 receptors, contributing to the maintenance of CSU lesions.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0220">Cutaneous mast cells chronically exposed to IL-33 (alarmin) have their FcɛRI-mediated degranulation attenuated; however, IL-33 can sensitize them to greater acute reactivity to MRGPRX2 receptor ligands.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a> IL-33's half-life is reduced by the action of proteases and histamine released during degranulation, which constitutes a negative feedback loop, controlling its actions.<a href="#bib0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0180"><sup>36</sup></a></p>
<section id="sec0030"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Inflammation and coagulation in CSU</h3>
