<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Minutes or up to 24 hours</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">12 hours or a few days</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">&gt;24 hours in the same place.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">&lt;24 hours</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Two days to weeks in the same place</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">&gt;24 hours and sometimes several days in the same place.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">&gt;24 hours</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Several days or weeks in the same places.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">&gt;24 hours and sometimes several days in the same place.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">&gt;24 hours.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1"><strong>Histopathology</strong></td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Nonspecific. Edema with Perivascular infiltrate of lymphocytes and polymorphonuclear cells. No epidermal changes.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">There may be neutrophilic perivascular infiltrate and neutrophilic epidermotropism</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">There may be neutrophilic perivascular infiltrate and neutrophilic epidermotropism (epidermis, hair follicles, and eccrine glands)</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Neutrophilic infiltration in the interstitium, between the collagen fibers, sometimes with a “single-file” appearance; without leukocytoclasis or vasculitis. No edema, and neutrophilic epidermotropism may be present.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Angiocentric infiltrate with or without interstitial spread. Leukocytoclasis and transmural polymorphonuclear cell infiltrate. Fibrin deposits in the blood vessel, red blood cell extravasation, and foci of thrombosis.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Mild spongiosis, perivascular infiltrate of lymphocytes and eosinophils, which may be in the superficial and middle dermis, is often mistaken for a drug reaction</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Variable, with spongiosis, parakeratosis, vacuolar degeneration of the basal layer in the epidermis, keratinocyte apoptosis, perivascular inflammatory infiltrate of lymphocytes, eosinophils and/or neutrophils.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">In lesions that are macroscopically free of vesicles or blisters in bullous pemphigoid, there may be eosinophilic spongiosis and eosinophils in the superficial dermis; in dermatitis herpetiformis, there may be neutrophils in the papillary dermis.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Discreet edema in the dermis with abundant eosinophils and “flame or blaze” figures, which are not pathognomonic of the disease.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Perivascular lymphomononuclear infiltrate with neutrophils and fewer eosinophils. Neutrophilic epidermotropism may be present.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl0025/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn1"><p>CSU, Chronic Spontaneous Urticaria; AIS, Autoinflammatory Syndromes (monogenic ones include cryopyrinopathies related to NLRP3 mutations such as CINCA/NOMID, Mückle-Wells Syndrome, familial cold urticaria autoinflammatory syndrome, NLRP-12 mutations, possibly hyper-IgD syndrome and familial Mediterranean fever); NUD, Neutrophilic Urticated Dermatosis; NUSI, Neutrophilic Urticaria with Systemic Symptoms; UV, Urticarial Vasculitis; UD, Urticated Dermatitis; Drug, urticated drug reactions; AIBD, Autoimmune Bullous Diseases with Atypical Presentation (especially bullous pemphigoid and dermatitis herpetiformis); AOSD; Adult-Onset Still's Disease; COBPD, Chronic Obstructive Bronchopulmonary Disease; ANA, Antinuclear Factor; CRP, C-Reactive Protein; ESR, Erythrocyte Sedimentation Rate.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="sec0070"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Therapeutic Basis and Perspectives</h2>
<p id="par0430">The goal of CSU treatment is to achieve complete disease control, with the absence of symptoms and signs of the disease, as reflected by the application and collection of PROMs (Patient Report Outcome Measure: UCT, Urticaria Control Test, in a four-question format, and the seven-day Urticaria Activity Score, UAS7), with the goal of achieving a UCT = 16 and UAS7 = 0 in patients, in addition to a DLQI equal to zero, if possible. The achievement of these less subjective parameters demonstrates that the therapeutic intervention restored normality to the patient's life, aiming to interrupt the natural course of the disease, treating it until it disappears definitively.<a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370">74</a>, <a href="#bib0385" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0385">77</a></p>
<p id="par0435">In this therapeutic journey, various factors can trigger CSU exacerbations throughout treatment, even with completely asymptomatic periods, such as those reported in the Chronic Urticaria Registry (CURE)<a href="#bib0445" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0445"><sup>89</sup></a>: stress (13.9%), exposure to NSAIDs (6.7%), infections (5.5%; especially viral infections of the upper respiratory tract, including COVID-19 and its vaccines),<a href="#bib0450" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0450">90</a>, <a href="#bib0455" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0455">91</a>, <a href="#bib0460" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0460">92</a> and, rarely, foods such as milk, fish, seafood, nuts, peanuts, spices, fruits, chocolate, and alcohol.<a href="#bib0385" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0385">77</a>, <a href="#bib0445" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0445">89</a></p>
<p id="par0440">The current international guidelines on urticaria provide a systemic treatment algorithm for CSU, which includes the use of daily second-generation antihistamines (anti-H1sg); Omalizumab (a humanized monoclonal anti-IgE IgG antibody) is prescribed monthly, and cyclosporine is prescribed daily, sometimes for long periods of years.<a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370">74</a>, <a href="#bib0385" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0385">77</a></p>
<p id="par0445">Old medications used to treat CSU before the advent of omalizumab, such as colchicine, dapsone, methotrexate, montelukast, hydroxychloroquine, H2-blocking antihistamines, and doxepin, have little scientific evidence due to the lack of randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies with relevant case studies. Therefore, they are not included in the recommendations of the 2022 international urticaria guideline.<a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370"><sup>74</sup></a> However, in patients who do not respond to the measures recommended in the consensus, these medications can be used exceptionally.<a href="#bib0465" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0465"><sup>93</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0450">At least 25% to 50% of patients with CSU, even using quadruple doses of H1sg antihistamines, continue to have their disease without complete control, notably patients who have severe disease, concomitant CSU, and induced CU, together with elevated levels of d-dimers and serum CRP.<a href="#bib0465" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0465">93</a>, <a href="#bib0470" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0470">94</a>, <a href="#bib0475" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0475">95</a>, <a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480">96</a>, <a href="#bib0485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0485">97</a> Although H1sg antihistamines constitute the first line of treatment for CSU, a 2016 systematic review found that standard doses (from the “on-label” package insert) controlled 38.3% of patients, and 63.2% of patients required doses increased up to 4× the standard dose.<a href="#bib0470" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0470"><sup>94</sup></a> There is evidence of effectiveness and, especially, safety in the use of up to four doses of H1s inhibitors as bilastine, cetirizine, levocetirizine, ebastine, fexofenadine, loratadine, desloratadine, mizolastine, and rupatadine<a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480"><sup>96</sup></a>; approximately 32% of patients who did not respond to H1s inhibitors are also partial responders or non-responders to omalizumab, reflecting the heterogeneity of CSU pathogenesis among different patients.<a href="#bib0465" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0465">93</a>, <a href="#bib0470" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0470">94</a>, <a href="#bib0475" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0475">95</a>, <a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480">96</a></p>
<p id="par0455">H1sg inhibitors act on histamine receptors as inverse agonists, rendering them inactive when bound to them, reversing histamine-induced vasodilation and increased capillary permeability, reducing the edema that constitutes urticaria/angioedema.<a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480"><sup>96</sup></a> Furthermore, blocking the action of histamine on receptors in blood vessels and sensory nerve endings indirectly reduces allergic inflammation by decreasing the accumulation of inflammatory cells that migrate to the skin and suppress the immune response to antigens through its action on NFκB, as well as on calcium channels, endothelial cells, dendritic cells, and lymphocytes, also preventing the recruitment of eosinophils, basophils, neutrophils, and other immune cells from the blood to the skin.<a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480"><sup>96</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0460">It should be noted that first-generation H1 antihistamines (H1fg – chlorpheniramine, diphenhydramine, hydroxyzine, promethazine, clemastine, doxepin, and cyproheptadine) were banned from the treatment of CSU due to their lower selectivity for H1 receptors, while also having affinity for muscarinic, serotonergic, and alpha-adrenergic receptors and cardiac potassium channels. These can cause side effects such as constipation, dry mouth, blurred vision, and can be potentially fatal. H1pg antihistamines are lipophilic drugs that cross the blood-cerebrospinal fluid barrier and, acting on the central nervous system, cause psychomotor changes, drowsiness, a comatose state, and decreased sleep quality (altering the REM sleep phase). These effects include side effects the day after nighttime use, decreased school and work performance, and an increased risk of drowsiness when driving.<a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480"><sup>96</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0465">Due to their reduced passage through the blood-cerebrospinal fluid barrier, H1sg inhibitors are indicated for the treatment of CSU. However, elderly patients (≥65 years) may be particularly susceptible to some H1sg inhibitors, such as cetirizine and loratadine, when the label-recommended doses are exceeded.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a>, <a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370">74</a>, <a href="#bib0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0480">96</a></p>
<p id="par0470">Omalizumab is indicated in addition to H1sg inhibitors when CSU is not controlled, as a second-line treatment, initially at the label-recommended doses of 300 mg subcutaneously every four weeks for at least six months.<a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370"><sup>74</sup></a> Its use is safe in pregnant women and is FDA category B in responding patients. A meta-analysis included randomized studies on the treatment of CSU/CIU, among patients older than 12 years of age, published between January 1, 2000, and July 31, 2021, recovering 854 articles, of which 14 met the inclusion criteria, comprising 577 participants using a placebo and H1 antihistamine and 1,209 participants with an active medication and an H1 antihistamine.<a href="#bib0485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0485"><sup>97</sup></a> It was observed that the effectiveness of omalizumab was dose-dependent, so that patients who are partial responders (to the on-label doses of 300 mg every four weeks) may benefit from increasing doses (450 mg or 600 mg every four weeks) or decreasing the interval to every two or three weeks between doses, which may be due to (i) the presence of higher serum levels of total IgE in these patients, since the most relevant pharmacological effect of omalizumab is to suppress the expression of IgE receptors. This action is due to the neutralization of free IgE in the blood, reducing it to almost zero, (ii) or the greater body weight of these patients.<a href="#bib0485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0485"><sup>97</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0475">Compared to the immunosuppressants analyzed in this meta-analysis (including cyclosporine, methotrexate, azathioprine, and hydroxychloroquine), omalizumab was generally associated with greater reductions in DLQI and UAS7 values, while also demonstrating a lower incidence of adverse events.<a href="#bib0485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0485"><sup>97</sup></a> Cyclosporine, in turn, was effective in many patients and can be used in omalizumab-refractory patients or in clinical settings where economic conditions do not allow access to omalizumab. However, it has greater adverse effects when compared to omalizumab.<a href="#bib0485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0485"><sup>97</sup></a> Another meta-analysis involving 67 real-life studies with CSU patients treated with omalizumab demonstrated complete and partial response rates of 72% and 18%, respectively, with an average adverse event rate of 4%, such as headache, fatigue, and injection-site reactions.<a href="#bib0490" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0490"><sup>98</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0480">In patients with a complete response to omalizumab, anti-H1sg and omalizumab may be tapered every three months, discontinued after 6 to 12 months to determine if CSU remission has occurred.<a href="#bib0385" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0385"><sup>77</sup></a> Alternative approaches to partial responses with omalizumab include using it in combination with immunosuppressive/immunomodulatory medications such as cyclosporine, dapsone, and colchicine, although the evidence for this use is very limited, or completely replacing it with cyclosporine, the third-line medication in the international urticaria guidelines.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a>, <a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370">74</a></p>
<p id="par0485">Cyclosporine is a T-cell immunosuppressive agent (inhibiting the production of IL-2, IL-3, IL-4, and TNF-α), which also inhibits the release of mediators from mast cells and basophils, and has been used for over three decades in the treatment of CU.<a href="#bib0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0055">11</a>, <a href="#bib0495" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0495">99</a> Kulthanan et al.<a href="#bib0495" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0495"><sup>99</sup></a> conducted a meta-analysis on the efficacy of cyclosporine in the treatment of CSU, involving 909 patients receiving doses between 1 and 5 mg/kg/day, having obtained a response rate to treatment after four weeks, at low doses (2-5 mg/kg/day) of 54% of patients, at eight weeks in 66% of them and at 12 weeks in 73% of patients, with patients presenting one or more adverse events with low doses (2-4 mg/kg/day) in 23% of them and 57% of those receiving moderate doses (4-5 mg/kg/day). Adverse events with the use of cyclosporine were dose-dependent, but the main ones were arterial hypertension and elevated serum creatinine in 6.2% with the use of low doses and 10.3% with moderate doses.<a href="#bib0495" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0495"><sup>99</sup></a> Other events included gastrointestinal symptoms (nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain), headache, hirsutism, infections, and paresthesia, observed in 13.9% of patients with low doses and 46.2% of those with moderate doses, with studies with these doses conducted in less than 24 weeks and less than 16 weeks of cyclosporine use, respectively.<a href="#bib0495" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0495"><sup>99</sup></a> It should be noted that prolonged use of cyclosporine, even at very low doses (&lt; 2 mg/kg/day), has been shown over five to ten years to be associated with abnormal glomerular filtration rates and a higher incidence of infections and malignancies among patients.<a href="#bib0495" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0495"><sup>99</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0490">Within the current therapeutic approach proposed by the international urticaria guide published in 2022,<a href="#bib0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0370"><sup>74</sup></a> there are, therefore, four medications with consensus regarding their effectiveness<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a>: (i) Second-generation antihistamines, which target promoting inverse agonism of H1 receptors and blocking the undesirable effects of histamine; (ii) Omalizumab, which targets IgE sequestration and thus allows the suppression of its receptors on mast cells and basophils, dissociating the IgE bound to these cells in its receptor (FcɛRI) and blocking the activation of these cells; (iii) Cyclosporine, which generally inhibits T-cell activation and the release of histamine from mast cells and basophils, and (iv) Glucocorticoids, whose continuous use is contraindicated due to their adverse effects. However, they are useful in exacerbations of CSU when used in 7-day cycles per month, as they do not act directly on mast cells, but inhibit the recruitment of eosinophils from the bone marrow to the peripheral blood and tissues, in addition to blocking the influx of all blood cells into the interstitium of the dermis.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0495">New knowledge about the pathogenesis of CSU has led to the discovery of molecules (biological or small molecules) that can act not only on mast cells and basophils, but also on the predominantly Th2 inflammatory cell infiltrate, which depends on alarmins (TSLP, IL-33, IL-25) produced by epithelial and endothelial cells, which lead Innate Immune Lymphoid Cells type 2 (ILC2) to stimulate, with IL-13 and IL-5, the activation of LTh2, as well as on prostaglandin D2 receptors (CRTH2) that activate lymphocytes, mast cells and basophils.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a> Receptors that stimulate the activation, differentiation, and survival of mast cells, such as c-Kit (CD117), are targeted by monoclonal antibodies such as barzolvolimab, blocking the action of its ligand, Stem Cell Factor (SCF). Other receptors that silence the mast cell and basophilic response, such as Siglec-8 (Sialic acid-binding immunoglobulin-like lectin-8) and Siglec-6, have been targeted by drugs with agonist function (anti-Siglec) to inhibit the function of these cells.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0500">There is a window of opportunity to act on this cascade of immune and inflammatory events, which also, to a lesser extent, involves LTh17 (for now, there are case reports with the use of secukinumab, an anti-IL-17, and tildrakizumab, an anti-IL-23).<a href="#bib0500" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0500">100</a>, <a href="#bib0505" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0505">101</a> Other biological medications undergoing phase II and III clinical trials, anti-IgEs, anti-TSLP, anti-IL-5 will soon have results published, in addition to dupilumab,<a href="#bib0510" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0510"><sup>102</sup></a> which is awaiting registration by regulatory agencies such as the EMA (European Union) and FDA (USA) for the treatment of CSU, but are already approved in Japan, the United Arab Emirates and Brazil for the treatment of the disease. Small molecules for oral use, such as Bruton's tyrosine kinase inhibitors (an intracellular pathway that activates degranulation and cytokine synthesis in mast cells and basophils, as well as a pathway that stimulates antibody synthesis in B cells), such as remibrutinib, fenibrutinib, and risalbrutinib, have been studied in phase II and III studies with promising results.<a href="#bib0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0265"><sup>53</sup></a></p></section><section id="sec0075"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Interleukin/cytokine receptor inhibitors or blockers</h2>
<ul id="lis0005" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li id="lsti0005">
<span class="label">1)</span><p class="display-inline" id="par0510">Dupilumab</p>
</li></ul>
<p id="par0515">Recent advances in understanding TH2 inflammation in CSU have opened new therapeutic horizons. Dupilumab is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that binds to IL-4Ra. This antibody inhibits IL-4 and IL-13-induced IL-4R signaling, reducing TH2 inflammation and potentially inhibiting IgE production in IL-4-treated B cells.<a href="#bib0515" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0515">103</a>, <a href="#bib0520" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0520">104</a> In a study that analyzed biopsies from patients with Chronic Spontaneous Urticaria (CSU), IL-4+ and IL-5+ cells were found in the lesional skin, with significant elevations compared to controls (patients without CSU). Furthermore, a marked increase in the number of IL-33+, IL-25+, and TSLP+ cells was observed in the dermis of lesional skin, compared to both non-lesional skin and the control group.<a href="#bib0285" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0285"><sup>57</sup></a> The increased expression of Th2-initiating cytokines in the lesional skin of patients with CSU suggests that innate pathways may play a role in the mechanism of hives formation.<a href="#bib0285" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0285"><sup>57</sup></a> Since Th2-initiating cytokines play a role in mast cell activation, inflammation, and vascular leakage, these findings may also have therapeutic implications for the use of dupilumab, for example.<a href="#bib0285" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0285"><sup>57</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0520">LIBERTY-CSU CUPID was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, 24-week trial.<a href="#bib0525" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0525"><sup>105</sup></a> This trial included two independent studies, CUPID A and CUPID B, with different populations and inadequate response to different prior therapies of the same duration. Study CUPID A included patients who had never used omalizumab, and CUPID B included patients intolerant to omalizumab or with an incomplete response.<a href="#bib0525" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0525"><sup>105</sup></a> In CUPID A, the combination of dupilumab with second-generation antihistamines provided significant and clinically relevant improvements at week 24, including pruritus, urticaria, and urticaria activity, compared to antihistamines alone.<a href="#bib0525" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0525"><sup>105</sup></a> In CUPID B, patients were defined as incomplete responders after three months of treatment with omalizumab, and in this group, there was a statistically significant improvement in pruritus and urticaria assessments after the use of dupilumab.<a href="#bib0525" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0525">105</a>, <a href="#bib0530" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0530">106</a> In December 2024, dupilumab was approved by ANVISA in Brazil for the treatment of CSU unresponsive to antihistamines in adolescents over 12 years of age and adults.</p>
<ul id="lis0010" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li id="lsti0010">
<span class="label">2)</span><p class="display-inline" id="par0525">Anti-IL-5</p>
</li></ul>
<p id="par0530">In CSU, IL-5 is responsible for attracting eosinophils to the skin, in addition to promoting their survival and proliferation.<a href="#bib0535" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0535"><sup>107</sup></a> One study demonstrated that patients with CSU have higher serum levels of IL-5 receptors than controls.<a href="#bib0540" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0540"><sup>108</sup></a> Another study detected higher concentrations of eosinophils in both lesional and non-lesional skin of individuals with CSU compared to controls.<a href="#bib0545" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0545"><sup>109</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0535">There are some case reports in which mepolizumab, an anti-IL5 monoclonal antibody, was successfully used for the treatment of CSU.<a href="#bib0550" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0550"><sup>110</sup></a> An open-label, exploratory, phase 1 study is currently being conducted to evaluate mepolizumab for the treatment of CSU.<a href="#bib0555" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0555"><sup>111</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0540">Benralizumab, an IL-5 receptor inhibitor (anti-IL-5Rα), underwent a phase 1 study with promising results in CSU.<a href="#bib0560" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0560"><sup>112</sup></a> However, these results were not corroborated in the randomized, placebo-controlled clinical trial. Therefore, its development program for CSU was discontinued.<a href="#bib0565" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0565"><sup>113</sup></a></p>
<ul id="lis0015" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li id="lsti0015">
<span class="label">3)</span><p class="display-inline" id="par0545">Anti-IL-17 – Secukinumab and Anti-IL-23 – Tildrakizumab</p>
</li></ul>
<p id="par0550">In a study of patients with CU, the levels of circulating inflammatory cytokines such as IL-6, TNF-α, and IL-12 were evaluated.<a href="#bib0570" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0570"><sup>114</sup></a> These were found at elevated levels in patients with chronic urticaria, in association with increased secretion of IL-2 and IL-17 after T-cell stimulation.<a href="#bib0300" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0300">60</a>, <a href="#bib0570" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0570">114</a> In another study, serum levels of IL-17, TNF-α, and IL-23 were evaluated in patients with CSU, in relation to the values of the urticaria activity scale, positive autoserum test in patients, and prick test results.<a href="#bib0575" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0575"><sup>115</sup></a> The concentration of these cytokines was significantly higher in patients with CSU compared to controls in all of these studies.<a href="#bib0300" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0300">60</a>, <a href="#bib0570" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0570">114</a>, <a href="#bib0575" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0575">115</a></p>
<p id="par0555">In a recent study, patients with severe CSU resistant to conventional treatment were treated off-label with anti-IL-17A (secukinumab – 150 mg weekly for four weeks and then every two weeks).<a href="#bib0580" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0580"><sup>116</sup></a> The improvement in disease activity (UAS7) was 55% at 30 days and 82% at three months. Discontinuation of treatment after six to eight months resulted in CSU relapse, indicating that anti-IL-17 would be necessary for a longer period.<a href="#bib0580" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0580"><sup>116</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0560">A case series reported the use of tildrakizumab, an anti-IL-23 agent, in three patients with high disease activity refractory to treatment with quadrupled doses of second-generation H1 antihistamines and omalizumab.<a href="#bib0585" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0585"><sup>117</sup></a> These patients received three doses of tildrakizumab 100 mg (at weeks 0, 4, and 12, the dose and interval used as licensed for psoriasis) as an off-label treatment, while maintaining antihistamines at quadruple doses. The treatment was well tolerated and led to significant improvements in disease activity and control in all patients after 12 weeks, with reductions in UAS7 and UCT scores and improvements in quality of life.<a href="#bib0585" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0585"><sup>117</sup></a> One patient achieved remission, another maintained adequate control with antihistamines, and one relapsed after stopping the drug.<a href="#bib0585" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0585"><sup>117</sup></a></p>
<ul id="lis0020" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li id="lsti0020">
<span class="label">4)</span><p class="display-inline" id="par0565">Anti-thymic stromal lymphopoietin (TSLP): Tezepelumab</p>
</li></ul>
<p id="par0570">Thymic stromal lymphopoietin (TSLP) is an epithelial-derived cytokine (alarmin) produced in response to environmental and pro-inflammatory stimuli. It is associated with the regulation of type 2 immunity, acting on dendritic cells, T and B cells, and innate immune lymphoid cells (ILC2).<a href="#bib0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0050"><sup>10</sup></a> TSLP expression is higher in the airways of patients with asthma, and its levels correlate with the expression of Th2 cytokines and chemokines and disease severity.<a href="#bib0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0050">10</a>, <a href="#bib0590" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0590">118</a></p>
<p id="par0575">Tezepelumab (AMG 157/MEDI9929) is a human IgG1 monoclonal antibody undergoing investigation that binds to TSLP, preventing its interaction with the TSLP receptor complex.<a href="#bib0590" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0590"><sup>118</sup></a> In a phase 2b study registered in Clinical Trials (INCEPTION study) with a 32-week observation period, comparing the effect of Tezepelumab (210 mg every four weeks or 420 mg every two weeks) versus omalizumab (300 mg every four weeks) or placebo, in patients refractory to optimized treatment with quadrupled doses of H1 antihistamines, a sustained treatment effect was observed in the Tezepelumab arms, not achieved with omalizumab or placebo.<a href="#bib0595" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0595"><sup>119</sup></a> Improvements in UAS7 values with the drug occurred with continued reductions in IL-5 and IL-13, regardless of changes in IgE levels.<a href="#bib0595" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0595"><sup>119</sup></a> In patients who never used anti-IgE, Tezepelumab treatment led to reductions in CSU activity and biomarkers after treatment discontinuation, suggesting a sustained effect of TSLP blocking after treatment cessation.<a href="#bib0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0050">10</a>, <a href="#bib0595" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0595">119</a></p>
<section id="sec0080"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Bruton's Tyrosine Kinase Inhibitors (BTK Inhibitors)</h3>
<ul id="lis0025" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li id="lsti0025">
<span class="label">1)</span><p class="display-inline" id="par0585">Remibrutinib</p>
</li></ul>
<p id="par0590">Remibrutinib (LOU064), an oral, covalent (stable-binding) BTK inhibitor, exhibits high selectivity and potency for this intracellular signaling pathway molecule in mast cells, basophils, and B cells. The high selectivity and tolerability of remibrutinib are likely due to its ability to bind to an inactive conformation of BTK.<a href="#bib0600" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0600"><sup>120</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0595">In a phase I study, remibrutinib was also effective in inhibiting basophil activation.<a href="#bib0605" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0605"><sup>121</sup></a> In a phase 2b clinical trial, the efficacy and safety of oral remibrutinib at various doses were demonstrated in patients with CSU inadequately controlled with second-generation antihistamines.<a href="#bib0610" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0610"><sup>122</sup></a> All doses of remibrutinib (10 mg/day, 35 mg/day, 100 mg/day, 10 mg twice daily, 25 mg twice daily, and 100 mg twice daily) significantly improved the signs and symptoms of CSU compared to placebo, with rapid clinical improvement observed in the first week (improvement of pruritus and hives) and maintained until week 12.<a href="#bib0610" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0610"><sup>122</sup></a> Greater overall efficacy was observed with remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily compared with placebo.<a href="#bib0610" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0610"><sup>122</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0600">Prior treatment with anti-IgE therapy did not affect the change in UAS7 score from baseline to week 12, as no relevant difference was observed between anti-IgE-experienced and naive patients in either the remibrutinib (any dose) or placebo arms.<a href="#bib0610" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0610"><sup>122</sup></a> Remibrutinib demonstrated a favorable safety profile at all tested doses.<a href="#bib0610" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0610"><sup>122</sup></a> Most Adverse Events (AEs) were mild or moderate, and the proportion of patients with at least one AE was similar across remibrutinib doses, indicating no dose-dependent pattern.<a href="#bib0610" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0610"><sup>122</sup></a> Similarly, the extension study of remibrutinib, observing patients who had UAS7 ≥ 16 in the previous 16-week study, remained in this study with remibrutinib at a dose of 100 mg twice daily for 52 weeks, in order to evaluate adverse events, the three most common of which were infections (30.9%) in the skin and subcutaneous tissue (26.8%) and gastrointestinal disorders (16.5%). UAS7 = 0 was achieved by 28.2% at week 4 and 55.8% at week 52, and UAS7 ≤ 6 by 52.5% at week 4 and 68.0% at week 52, demonstrating the efficacy and safety of this medication.<a href="#bib0615" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0615"><sup>123</sup></a></p>
<ul id="lis0030" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li id="lsti0030">
<span class="label">2)</span><p class="display-inline" id="par0605">Fenebrutinib</p>
</li></ul>
<p id="par0610">Fenebrutinib is an oral, highly selective, and reversible BTK inhibitor that blocks IgE-mediated histamine release from mast cells <em>in vitro</em>. In a phase 1 study, fenebrutinib inhibited IgE-mediated basophil activation in healthy volunteers. Thus, BTK inhibition by fenebrutinib may also interrupt the production of autoantibodies that activate FcεRI in CSU. A phase 2b, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study involving 134 participants in 57 days of treatment, distributed in a pilot cohort (cohort 1, dose of 200 mg, twice a day) and a variable-dose cohort (cohort 2, subdivided into doses of 50 mg daily, 150 mg daily and 200 mg twice a day), observed the efficacy and safety of fenebrutinib, compared to placebo, in adult patients with CSU for more than six months, symptomatic despite treatment with H1 antihistamines at an optimized dose (up to four times the approved dose in the package insert).<a href="#bib0620" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0620"><sup>124</sup></a> The rapid onset of efficacy suggests that the main mechanism of action of the drug in CSU is inhibition of the FcεRI signal via inhibition of BTK in mast cells and basophils.<a href="#bib0625" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0625"><sup>125</sup></a> Fenebrutinib controlled CSU in patients with type IIb autoimmunity at all tested doses.<a href="#bib0620" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0620">124</a>, <a href="#bib0625" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0625">125</a></p>
<p id="par0615">However, at lower doses, patients with type IIb autoimmunity experienced greater benefit than patients without type IIb-associated markers.<a href="#bib0625" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0625"><sup>125</sup></a> Patients with type IIb autoimmunity may be more sensitive to BTK inhibition, while patients with type I autoallergy may derive additional clinical benefit from higher levels of pathway inhibition to achieve maximal efficacy.<a href="#bib0625" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0625"><sup>125</sup></a> The primary endpoint of achieving a UAS7 ≤ 7 was achieved with the 200 mg twice daily and 150 mg daily doses, but not with the 50 mg daily dose of fenebrutinibe. Asymptomatic and reversible grade 2 and 3 transaminase elevations occurred with the 150 mg daily and 200 mg twice daily doses (two participants in each group).<a href="#bib0625" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0625"><sup>125</sup></a></p></section><section id="sec0081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">MGPRX2 Receptor Inhibitor</h3>
<p id="par0625">In addition to the high-affinity IgE receptor (FcεRI), mast cells express numerous G-protein-coupled receptors (GPCRs), which can be activated by neuropeptides, major basic protein (MBP), and eosinophil peroxidase (EPO), among others, culminating in mast cell degranulation.<a href="#bib0630" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0630"><sup>126</sup></a> Mas-related G-protein-coupled receptor X2 (MRGPRX2) is a GPCR expressed with some selectivity by mast cells and can be present both in the plasma membrane and intracellularly.<a href="#bib0630" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0630"><sup>126</sup></a> MRGPRX2 expression is increased in the skin of individuals with CSU when compared with healthy controls.<a href="#bib0635" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0635"><sup>127</sup></a> Fujisawa et al. <a href="#bib0635" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0635"><sup>127</sup></a> also observed eosinophil infiltration in the lesional skin in seven out of nine individuals with CSU. The authors also demonstrated that proteins originating from eosinophils (MBP and EP) are capable of inducing histamine release; this effect is inhibited in cells in which MRGPRX2 is silenced.<a href="#bib0635" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0635"><sup>127</sup></a> An oral MRGPRX2 inhibitor (EP262) is currently recruiting for a phase 2 clinical trial for CSU, a study called CALM-CSU.<a href="#bib0640" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0640"><sup>128</sup></a></p></section><section id="sec0085"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Tyrosine kinase receptor (KIT) inhibitors (c-KIT ligand inhibitors)</h3>
<p id="par0630">KITs (CD117) are transmembrane receptors for Stem Cell Factor (SCF) and are present on progenitor cell lines.<a href="#bib0645" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0645"><sup>129</sup></a> In the case of mast cells, even mature cells continue to express KIT at high levels.<a href="#bib0645" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0645"><sup>129</sup></a> When activated by SCF, KIT promotes cell survival, migration, and the secretion of several inflammatory mediators.<a href="#bib0645" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0645"><sup>129</sup></a> In mast cells, KIT activation by SCF induces degranulation.<a href="#bib0645" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0645"><sup>129</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0635">Barzolvolimab is a humanized monoclonal antibody that binds to the KIT extracellular domain on mast cells, preventing SCF binding and subsequent mast cell activation.<a href="#bib0650" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0650"><sup>130</sup></a> In a randomized phase 2 clinical trial for the treatment of CSU, barzolvolimab demonstrated a significant improvement in the UAS7 score compared to the placebo group, over a 12-week follow-up.<a href="#bib0655" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0655"><sup>131</sup></a> No major adverse effects were observed in this same study.<a href="#bib0655" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0655"><sup>131</sup></a> In Chronic inducible urticaria (CIndU), specifically symptomatic dermographism and cold urticaria, there was complete resolution of the condition in 95% of patients after a single infusion of barzolvolimab, over a 12-week follow-up.<a href="#bib0660" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0660"><sup>132</sup></a> Furthermore, there was a significant reduction in the number of mast cells in the patients' skin, as well as a reduction in tryptase levels and an increase in soluble SCF after treatment.<a href="#bib0660" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0660"><sup>132</sup></a> Barzolvolimab is currently being investigated in phase 3 clinical trials for CSU and is considered a promising treatment for CSU (EMBARQ-CSU2 study).<a href="#bib0665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0665"><sup>133</sup></a></p>
<p id="par0640">Briquilimab is another anti-KIT monoclonal antibody that is being evaluated for the treatment of CSU, but is still recruiting participants.<a href="#bib0670" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0670"><sup>134</sup></a></p></section><section id="sec0090"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Anti-Siglecs Medications</h3>
<p id="par0645">Siglecs, short for sialic-acid-binding immunoglobulin-like lectins, are a family of receptors expressed on mast cells, eosinophils, and basophils.<a href="#bib0675" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0675"><sup>135</sup></a> They have an inhibitory function, acting as immune system checkpoints.<a href="#bib0675" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0675"><sup>135</sup></a> Siglec-6 and -8 have been explored as therapeutic targets in allergic diseases.<a href="#bib0675" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0675"><sup>135</sup></a> Siglec-8 induces eosinophil apoptosis and inhibits mast cell activation; siglec-6 appears to promote even more potent inhibitory signals in mast cells.<a href="#bib0675" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0675">135</a>, <a href="#bib0680" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0680">136</a></p>
<p id="par0650">Lirentelimab (AK002), an anti-Siglec-8 monoclonal antibody, has been evaluated for the treatment of CSU. Despite showing promising results in an initial proof-of-concept study, the subsequent phase 2 clinical trial did not meet the primary endpoint and was terminated early by the manufacturer.<a href="#bib0685" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0685"><sup>137</sup></a> A humanized IgG1 anti-Siglec-6 monoclonal antibody (AK006) is currently being investigated in clinical trials for the treatment of CSU.<a href="#bib0690" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib0690"><sup>138</sup></a></p></section></section><section id="sec0095"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions</h2>
<p id="par0655">Urticaria, and CSU in particular, is a disease with rapidly expanding knowledge regarding its pathophysiological aspects, which are the basis for the development of new drugs for its management.</p>
<p id="par0660">Many genetic aspects that may predispose individuals to the disease, the exogenous factors that play a variable and individual role, the recognition of specific endotypes (autoallergic and autoimmune), the central role of mast cell degranulation, the important role of different cells and mediators, comprising diverse mechanisms in the pathogenesis, and biomarkers have been established; however, there are still gaps to be addressed.</p>
<p id="par0665">Studies on new drugs for the treatment of CSU reflect the multiplicity of pathogenic pathways. Once clinical trials demonstrate efficacy and safety, as has already been the case with omalizumab and others, regimens with immunobiologicals and/or small molecules will likely be considered alternatives to current treatments, especially in cases of resistance to conventional management.</p>
<p id="par0670">The fight for a better quality of life for patients with CSU is constant, and science is on our side, reminding us that promising therapies must be accompanied by concerns about accessibility, correct indication, and clinical monitoring.</p></section><section id="sec1100"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Editor</h2>
<p id="par1125">Ana Maria Roselino.</p></section><section id="sec0100"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Financial support</h2>
